## Path of the Zealot

The Path of the Zealot was a primal path for those warriors that were said to have distinguished souls destined to battle for all eternity. Known individually as zealots, these barbarians fought with such determined faith that they channeled divine power unto themselves when they entered into a barbarous rage.

Culture
Barbarians that walked the path of the zealot were often found among the faithful of Bane, Gruumsh, Tempus, and Tyr.

Abilities
After beginning down the path of the zealot, these barbarians could channel divine power―in the form of necrotic or holy energy―in the first blow they landed in battle while enraged.

While enraged, more battle-hardened zealots could shrug off effects that would incapacitate lesser warriors. They learned how to inspire zeal in others, bolstering their allies' combat prowess for a short while.

The most veteran zealots could shrug off mortal wounds and keep fighting in a rage of fury. Their injuries would only catch up to them once their rage ended, and it was only then that their life could be claimed by the gods.

## Path of the Totem Warrior

The Path of the Totem Warrior was a primal path for barbarians that involved spiritual attunement to supernatural beings. Known as totem warriors or wildheart barbarians, these tribal warriors adopted spirit animals as their patrons, who acted as their sacred guides in life and protective guardians in battle.

Description
These barbarians often adopted physical characteristics of their totem animal. It was not unheard of for the skin of these barbarians to thicken like hide, or even for their eyes to change color.

Culture
The Path of the Totem Warrior was believed to be something of a mystical journey for barbarians. The totem spirit to which they attuned was often linked to their specific tribe. The Uthgardt barbarians of the North nearly always walked this primal path, in contrast to their berserker brethren among the Reghedmen tribes of the Frozenfar.

While not unheard of, it was rather unusual for a barbarian to take on more than one animal spirit as their guide. Among the spirits most commonly adopted by totem warriors were Bear, Eagle, Wolf, Elk, and Tiger. Some more "civilized" warriors adopted other animals as their totem spirits, including crows, giant spiders, and even flying snakes.

The Uthgardt venerated the five primary animal spirits, each of which was associated with one or more of their tribes. Barbarians of Blue Bear and Thunderbeast tribes each adopted Bear as the totem spirit, though the latter also attuned specifically to Tiger. Similarly, the Black Raven and Sky Pony tribes were closely bonded to Eagle, but the latter took on the bestial aspects of Elk.

Possessions
When attuning to their spirit, they crafted a physical totem in the form of jewelry or a handheld item, that represented the spirit animal itself. These totems were made using bones, claws, feathers, or other pieces of the animal's body.

Abilities
As they forged their bond with the natural world, totem warriors learned how to perform rituals that granted them greater empathy and even allowed them to speak with wild animals. More veteran barbarians could even learn to commune with nature to gain insight over the physical world around them.

These warriors learned to channel the spirit of their totem animal during their rages in battle. Over time they were even granted magical boons by their spirit animal in the form of bestial aspects and totemic attunement, the latter of which manifested during their rages.

## Pale Justice

Pale Justice was a holy avenger crafted with the sole purpose to destroy evil throughout the Realms.

Description

Despite its powerful properties, Pale Justice was notably plain in appearance.

Powers
The sword was blessed by the god Tyr with a +4 enchantment. It struck more true and inflicted more grievous wounds against any malevolent beings it struck.

Whomever wielded it became immune to a number of spells that instilled fear and horror within others.

History

Pale Justice was originally forged as a non-magical blade by by a Tyrran smithy named Renold, some time during or before the mid–13 century DR. Renold humbly offered the non-magical sword to Edan, a devout acolyte in the church of Tyr. Edan and other priest of the local temple beseeched Tyr to bless the sword with divine power, but saw no change in its form or essence. All but one of the priests gave up on the effort, save for Edan, who continued his prayers for 30 years.

When a group of visiting priests of Helm mocked Edan for his futile prayers, the Tyrran priests lifted the blade from upon the altar. He stated that Tyr's work with the sword had been completed years ago. A guardsman of the Helmite priests drew his own, heavily-ornamented sword and ridiculed Pale Justice. Edan gently tapped the other sword with Pale Justice and saw that the overly-adorned blade break in the guardsman's hand.

Edan commissioned a simple, leather-wrapped wooden handle for the sword. He bestowed it upon a young paladin with the request he always bear in mind the profound graciousness humankind is capable of.

As of the, the sword remained in the possession of an unknown individual who died within the Artisan's District of Dorn's Deep.

## Patience

Patience was an assassin who served as the bodyguard for a noble-born druid named Belvyre during the late 15 century DR.

Personality
While Patience could be considered a no-nonsense individual, she had a rather dry wit. She remained ever vigilant in difficult situations and was already ready for a fight.

Possessions
She wore a suit of leather armor and carried with her four daggers.

Relationships

Patience had a strong bond with Belvyre, having acted as his personal guard his entire life. She was entirely loyal to the young druid and he considered her a dear friend. Belvyre constantly made cutesy nicknames for Patience―such as Pay-Pay or Patty Cake―several of which she had to veto outright.

Throughout their long history together, Belvyre always seemed to get them into peril, and Patience always managed to keep them safe.

History
Some time during or after the, Patience and Belvyre traveled to the Ten Towns of Icewind Dale to help with the natural disaster known as the Everlasting Rime. When everlasting darkness spread across the region, crops withered and food became scarce. As a skilled herbalist, Belvyre strived to grow new food types and find new sources of warmth. While in town, they met the travelers Saarvin, Runa, and Amos, and heard rumors about an underground city that housed powerful magics. Belvyre grew excited at the prospect, and Patience, as always, accompanied him for protection.

The group set out across the snowscape of Icewind Dale, and were eventually forced to take refuge in a cave to keep safe from the elements. Unfortunately, their refuge was assailed by a group of frost giant skeletons and the group became scattered when Amos turned on the others, in order to kidnap Belvyre for his magic power. When Runa came to, her mind was altered by a deposit of chardalyn and she attacked Saarvin. Patience attempted to intervene, but barbarians from the Blue Bear tribe intervened, and took everyone captive. Fortunately, Patience and Saarvin were able to talk their way out of the situation and were released.

Patience, Saarvin, Runa and some of the Blue Bear warriors ventured into a cave network within the Spine of the World mountains to rescue Belvyre. When the group caught up to Amos and Belvyre, Patience got some revenge by punch Amons square in the jaw. Belvyre urged them to stop fighting and work together to to escape the caverns, which were erupting with volcanic activity. As magma burst around them, Patience was bowled over and knocked unconscious. Amos sacrificed himself for Belvyre, so the little young halfling could in turn rescue Patience. In the end, all the members of the party save Amos made it to safety, and uncovered a pathway to the lost city they searched for.

## Path of the ancestral guardian

The Path of the Ancestral Guardian was a primal path for barbarians from clans and tribes that held special reverence for their deceased ancestors.

Description
These barbarians decorated their bodies with elaborate tattoos that honored the lives and deeds of their forebears. These tattoos often depicted epic battles against monstrous creatures and other sagas that were shared down through the generations.

Culture
Ancestral guardians believed that long-dead warriors from their tribes continued their afterlife as spirits, and passed down wisdom and guidance to their descendents in times of need.

Abilities
Barbarians that walked the path of the Ancestral Guardian learned to tap into the spirit world in battle. Whenever they entered into their barbarous rage, spectral warriors appeared and hindered their foes in battle.

Experienced barbarians could form a spiritual shield and protected themselves and their allies. Over time, ancestral guardians learned how to turn their foes' attacks against them, causing the spirits of their ancestors to retaliate against an enemy when they were struck in battle.

Veteran ancestral guardians learned how to not only conjure the spirits of their forebears, but communicate with them as well. They could consult with their ancestor to better understand the potential outcome of an event or impose upon them to glean insight to a location beyond their senses.

## Paper of forms

Paper of forms was an enchanted sheet of paper that could be transformed into various objects with an application of origami skills.

Description
Paper of forms was a colored sheet of paper.

Powers
A person skilled in origami could fold paper of forms into a variety of origami forms. With an utterance of a command word, folded paper transformed into a fully functional life-sized version of the origami piece. There were limits on what could be created with paper of forms. Only non-magical items could be created, both man-made and items created by nature, as well as mundane non-intelligent creatures. A single paper of forms could create an object not bigger than a small house.

The resulting creation radiated faint magic but otherwise was indistinguishable from what it was imitating. However, it was vulnerable to fire: mundane flames almost always set the created item on fire and magical fire did the job one hundred percent of the time.

Paper of forms returned to its paper form only when the owner voiced the second command word, and it was reusable. If the origami was ever unfolded, paper of forms lost all of its magic and became useless.

Creation
A sheet of paper of forms could be created by an experienced maker of wondrous items with the use of animate objects and major creation spells.

## Partisan

A partisan was a member of the spear family of polearms, being particularly similar to the spetum and ranseur in design,The partisan could possibly be considered a "combination weapon," what with its small axeheads, but since its primary purpose is thrusting it's considered a member of the spear family. to the point that it was not uncommon to confuse them.

Description
Partisans consisted of spear, with a long reach courtesy of a shaft that measured  in length. The heads of these spears were double-edged and large enough to support engraving and ornamentation. Directly below this spear head, two flukes or "axe-like spikes" protruded diagonally from the base.

Weaponology
This weapon was primarily used for thrusting at and piercing opponents', but the two flukes protruding from below the spearhead gave the weapon greater utility of cutting and slashing attacks. They also improved the partisan's penetrating potential, offered a defensive potential to the user, and could be used to catch and break an opponent's weapon.

Availability
Places in the Realms where one could purchase partisans included:
 The Weaponers of Cormyr.

 The Ravenian merchant Bendekar had multiple partisans in his home's expansive weapons collection.

## Pact of the Tome

The Pact of the Tome was a type of pact between a warlock and their patron in which the former was gifted a grimoire of spells, known as a Book of Shadows, in recognition of faithful service.

Abilities
Warlocks who pursued this type of pact had the ability to conduct an hourlong ritual to manifest a Book of Shadows (and in the process dispose of a lost or damaged older tome) which granted them access to expanded magical knowledge.

Some warlocks could also manifest new pages in the book, upon which others could write their names in order to bind themselves to the warlock in specific ways. These might include granting the warlock the ability to stay in contact with them by inscribing a message on the page (which would be transmitted to the signatory via a sending spell) or by offering special protection should they become grievously injured. A warlock could freely erase a name from their Book of Shadows, and was limited in the number of names that they could manage at a time.

Additionally, some bearers of a Book of Shadows gained the eldritch ability to stay awake in perpetuity, perhaps merely poring over their Book of Shadows rather than needing to sleep. Such warlocks also became immune to any magical compulsion to sleep.

Possessions
The signature Book of Shadows contained cantrips that no warlock might otherwise know without the generosity of their patron. The warlock was able to cast these spells as long as the Book was on their person. Some warlocks chose to emphasize the value of their tome as a book of ancient secrets, and their patron granted them additional ritual spells and permission to inscribe other rituals into their book as they saw fit.

The appearance and design of a Book of Shadows was determined by the patron, and might also be used to enumerate the costs that the warlock would have to endure in exchange for greater power from that patron. The Book disintegrated into ash if the warlock was slain.

History
Farideh, a tiefling warlock active around Waterdeep in the late 15 century DR, was said to carry a Book of Shadows given to her by her patron. She used it to drain the life from her enemies.

Appendix
Appearances

References

## Pain touch

Pain touch was an arcane spell from the school of necromancy that allowed a wizard to induce extreme pain with a touch.

Powers
The caster could affect a single target once they touched an exposed part of their skin. The wracking pain made the victim more clumsy and easier to strike in combat.

Components
In addition to verbal components, this spell required as material components a needle and the finger from a burned glove to cast.

History
This spell was found within the Book of Shadows, a tome based upon the oration of the necromancer Nebt Bhakau of Zakhara.

## Partic Thistle

Lord Partic Thistle was an ex-adventurer noble of the Thistle family, and the Registrant General of Cormyr in the mid-to-late 14  century DR. He resided at Thistleflame Keep in Suzail.

Personality
Lord Thistle was a kind and generous man who loved hosting guests at Thistleflame. He was fiercely dedicated to his King and country, and saw the promotion of adventuring companies as a way to advance the safety and welfare of the adventurers and the realm as a whole.

He had a morbid fascination with the past, especially his family's, an eccentricity which was gossiped about by society. He was extremely fond of sneaking down to his family's crypt and wandering about the tombs of his ancestors.

Abilities
Lord Thistle was an adept fighter and swordsman, having adventured in his youth.

He possessed an eidetic memory able to perfectly recall almost anything he witnessed. This was a great boon to his work as Registrant General, as he memorized every detail he could about the adventuring companies he dealt with, far beyond what official documentation recorded.

Possessions
He casually wore bracers of defense and carried a rapier, and bore the Thistle signet ring. In case of need, he kept a magical suit of +2 full plate armor and a +4 defender broadsword in his bedroom. He also had a +1 flame tongue longsword and a +3 vorpal longsword that he displayed in the Grand Hall, trophies of his ancestor's victories.

Activities
As Registrant General of Adventuring Companies, Lord Thistle was ultimately responsible for the state of adventurers in the realm. From his office in the Royal Court, he oversaw the dispensation of charters to groups of adventurers in Cormyr, as well as potentially withdrawing the charters and penalizing the groups. He monitored the behavior and progress of these parties, judging whether intervention was needed to correct them through punishment or reward. Although rogue adventurers were typically handled by the Purple Dragons, he would delegate lower-risk missions to trustworthy adventuring groups.

Relationships
Lord Thistle carried on a romantic engagement with the barmaid Annalise at the Bubbling Bulwark, but feared the derision his peers would lavish him with for marrying such a lowborn commoner. This relationship was much-gossiped about in Suzail's noble circles.

His closest friend and confidant was his butler, Moorefield, who he entrusted with the most vital aspects of his life.

Despite her best efforts, his maid Tami was endlessly frustrated by his refusal to respond to her flirtations.

History
In 1369 DR, he hosted a party at Thistleflame for King Azoun Obarskyr IV and a number of other distinguished guests, hoping to raise the reputation of adventurers in the realm. Instead, he was kidnapped by the Emerald Swords adventuring company and replaced by the doppelganger Xixis. The Emerald Swords were working for Zhentil Keep and intended to spirit him away for interrogation, as his eidetic memory made him the single greatest repository of information on adventuring groups in Cormyr and possibly the Realms. Xixis was to impersonate him for a time so that his kidnapping would go unnoticed until the Emerald Swords were well away from the scene.

The plan immediately went awry after the Swords hid Lord Partic in his family crypt. Xixis acted recklessly in his new guise, having come to enjoy the luxuries and benefits of his noble persona far too much. Deciding that Xixis would be revealed and spoil the whole plan, the Emerald Swords killed their ally, hoping to throw suspicions on the entire body of visiting nobles and adventurers at the estate, and prevent any pursuit for their kidnapping by having the target already apparently dead.

This new plan also quickly went wrong, as his butler Moorefield happened to check on him almost immediately afterwards. Finding what seemed to his murdered master, the alarm was raised and the entire estate detained by the Purple Dragons. Despite the array of eccentric and suspicious characters at Thistleflame, the investigation that followed uncovered the Emerald Sword's involvement, with the kidnappers detained and Lord Thistle recovered unharmed. In thanks to his rescuers, he awarded them a handsome sum of money as well as the Seal of Iron, part of the Four from Cormyr.

Later that year, he hired adventurers (possibly the same ones) to investigate the status of the Wolfmasters adventuring company, which was late in paying for its charter. The trail led from Thunderstone to Orvaskyte Keep in the Vast Swamp, and the discovery that the Wolfmasters were not delinquent, but dead.

Trivia
Partic Thistle is likely a reference to the Partick Thistle Football Club, in Glasgow, Scotland.

## Patricia

Patricia was the proprietor of the Den of the Seven Vales tavern and an inn in the City of Coin, Athkatla, in the mid-to-late 14 century DR.

Description
Patricia was a poultry woman with a large personality who harbored disdain for males, especially for loud, brash, and stupid men. She was always glad to see women patrons enter her establishment.

Patricia did not care for gossip and complained that her tavern's customers yacked about other people's business more than the lippiest fishwife.

Relationships
Patricia employed Alatelo De Bonito, a wizard security guard who also served as her financial advisor. The man was an aspiring bard and often tried to perform his laments to unenthused customers and Patricia's irritation. She often threatened to fire Alatelo for his whaling.

History
By the, Patricia's husband Richard was gone, and she was left a widow. The woman inherited the Seven Vales from Richard, who was responsible for the inn's queer name. Patricia's husband employed belly dancers, all of whom were fired when she took over, but Patricia did not have the hart to change the establishment's name by much, simply calling it the Den of the Seven Vales. Patricia was annoyed that the inn and tavern's regulars were drunk males who used to frequent the place before the ownership change. She believed that the drunks never even realized that the dancers were long gone from the Den.

## Pale Claw

The Pale Claw was a bandit gang that operated throughout the Dessarin Valley in the Savage Frontier for a time during the late 15 century DR. They performed the bidding of their secretive, undead master.

Organization
The bandits served the great white dracolich Glyrgoth, who used them to accumulate more and more riches.

Activities
Members of the gang spent their time stealing valuables and weaponry from innocent folks of the North. They used their ill-gotten gains to grow the hoard of their undead draconic master.

Tactics
The gang would move into an area, set up operations and completely terrorize a single town or settlement. They stole all they could from its people, subjugated them to forced labor, and amassed weaponry for further operations.

Base of Operations
For a time, the Pale Claw operated out of the abandoned mine in the Savage Frontier. Glyrgoth made its lair within the same mines.

History

Some time during or before 1490s DR, the Pale Claw took over the small town of Womford, and stole nearly all the wealth and valuables from its residents. To redirect their attention elsewhere, the barkeep of the local tavern―a dwarf by the name of Grunnald―joined the Pale Claw. He ascended as their new 'bandit king' and enjoyed some success, directing the Pale Claw to focus on wealthy folks of the North. Once Grunnald offered all the information found useful by the gang, they had no more use of him. The mortal 'bandit king' became a puppet leader, a figurehead they could parade around to distract from the Pale Claw's true master Glyrgoth.

Later on, an adventuring party composed of Edgin Darvis, Holga Kilgore, Simon Aumar, and Thelvyn Kip traveled into Womford, and offered to aid the plight of its people. The group gathered some weapons and equipment and confronted the Pale Claw gang. While they managed to destroy a few of the Pale Claw's undead minions, they were ultimately unsuccessful. The adventurers were then abducted and taken to the Pale Claw's hideout in the abandoned mines. There they met Grunnald, and came to understand the true nature of the Pale Claw's operations.

Unfortunately for the Pale Claw, the heroes carried out a daring escape in the mines. They broke themselves out of imprisonment, freed the gang's prisoners, and even managed to steal back some of the riches accumulated by Glyrgoth. Just before the group it out of the mines, Glygorth appeared before them and recovered the stolen treasure. The adventurers only made away with a single porcelain figurine of a boar. The whole ordeal upended the bandits' operations however: the people of Womford had given all they could and Glyrgoth was poised to move on to another target. The dracolich and her followers moved on to better prospects.

Members
The gang attracted members form various races. Some of them even kept a small group of skeletal undead among their enforcers.

## Pact of the Blade

The Pact of the Blade was a type of pact between a warlock and their patron in which the warlock received a distinctive weapon.

Abilities
The warlock could create the magical pact weapon in their hand at will, with its type chosen each time it was created. They could only create melee weapons, but more veteran warlocks could master a certain eldritch invocation that allowed them to creating bows and crossbows of various forms. Moreover, such a weapon became lightly enchanted and could be used as a spell focus. If the pact weapon was out of the warlock's reach for more than a minute, it would vanish, but the warlock could simply summon another. It would also vanish if the warlock willed it, summoned a different weapon, or died.

With other invocations, a warlock's pact weapon could drink the life-force of those it struck to inflict more serious injuries, or they could move and attack twice as often with it than was normal.

History
In the 15 century DR, Pact of the Blade warlocks could be found in parts of the Sword Coast.

## Pact blade

Pact blades were weapons that could also serve as a warlock's spell focus. This versatility meant that they were a preferred and much-sought-after implement among warlocks.

Description
A pact blade was typically a dagger, but could be any lightweight bladed weapon, such as a sickle. Although they were valued both as wickedly sharp blades and as useful spellcasting implements, they were somewhat awkward to wield in battle when also being used as spellcasting foci.

Some pact blades were said to be so suffused with the energies of the warlock patron to which they were bonded that they could confer feelings, sensations, and even powers associated with that patron onto the wielder.

Powers
As magical weapons, pact blades were more precise and more devastating than ordinary blades, especially when a target was struck in a vital area.

As an implement for assisting with warlock spellcasting, pact blades conferred greater potency to spells, invocations, and other warlock powers. They could also be used to confer a curse onto a warlock's foes: if a cursed individual attempted to strike the warlock, they were punished by receiving a magical wound as if they had been slashed by the pact blade.

The more potent the enchantment of a pact blade, the more powerful were both its deadliness as a weapon and its usefulness as a spellcasting focus.

History
Pact blades were said to have been in use since ancient times and across all corners of Faerûn, from Netheril in the north to Chult in the south, and from Waterdeep in the west to Mulhorand in the east.

They were favored and highly desirable among warlocks as of the late 15 century DR, however even minor pact blades could require money and influence to acquire. They were known to be available in Baldur's Gate, Neverwinter, New Velar, and Suzail.

## Path of Stones

Path of Stones is the title of an unpublished novel that was planned to be written by David Cook circa 1989. It was to be set in Kara-Tur and likely would've been the eleventh novel of the Forgotten Realms line.

Development
According to D&D researcher Shannon Appelcline, David "Zeb" Cook (who had worked on much of the Oriental Adventures setting) had proposed a Kara-Tur novel for Forgotten Realms. It was being considered by Jean Black, the head of TSR, Inc.'s fiction department at the time. The characters of Path of Stones were presented in Hall of Heroes in 1989, with write-ups by Mike Pondsmith. These were intended as a preview of the novel, alongside the characters of the then-forthcoming The Avatar Trilogy.

Cover art by Jim Holloway was also intended, depicting the Earth Spider clutching an unconscious woman, likely Onoye, and a warrior presumed to be Sanehiro. This artwork was included in the Forgotten Realms 1990 Calendar under the title "Path of Stones" with a quote from Dragon Lord Mei Lung about the Celestial Bureaucracy and was later used for two unrelated AD&D Trading Cards in 1991: Goblin Spider and Seiroku Ashida.

However, plans for Path of Stones were halted for various unspecified reasons. Appelcline speculates it was lost or shelved when Mary Kirchoff replaced Black as head of TSR's fiction, as is common in publishing.

Nevertheless, the desire for a Kara-Tur novel remained, leading to The Empires trilogy beginning in 1990, with Cook writing Horselords, although not set in Kara-Tur, and Troy Denning writing Dragonwall, which would be the sole Kara-Tur novel. A few of the characters of Path of Stones also made cameos in the adventures Ronin Challenge and Test of the Samurai earlier that same year.

Index
The following index is taken from Hall of Heroes and is included here for reference, but may not reflect plans for the novel itself.

## Path of the storm herald

The path of the storm herald was a primal path taken by barbarians who chose to channel the primal magic of nature into their bodies during a rage. Individual barbarians that took this path in life were known as storm heralds.

Description
While in their rage, a storm herald became a tempest of natural magic, which swirled around their body in a dramatic display.

Culture
They were among the highest-regarded barbarians of their given tribes. They often trained in understanding and harnessing primal magic alongside rangers and druids.

Some among their ranks chose to dwell in the harshest and most remote locations, such as isolated glaciers, barren deserts, and storm-wracked coastal lands.

Abilities
Early during their training, storm heralds learned to create an aura around their body that resembled a magical tempest. This aura behaved differently depending on the habitat with which the barbarian most closely identified. Heralds of the deserts conjured blistering heat, while those who harnessed the power of icy tundra solicited ice spirits to their aid.

As a barbarian's very soul became closer in tune with nature, they acquired inner strength and resilience to the elements.

The most powerful storm heralds could harness their storm auras to the greatest effect. They could extend their own elemental resilience to others and even turn their auras on their foes. Heralds of the sea could use their aura to knock down enemies as if struck by a wave, while those from the cold lands could freeze them where they stood.

## Parka

Parkas was clothing used to keep warm in cold climates.

Description
A parka was a practical garment that consisted of a one-piece jacket and hood designed to be slipped over the head like a sweater. It extended just below the waist for optimal coverage. The interior was lined with seal skin, providing insulation, while the outer layer was made of fur. The hood had a rounded shape and hung loosely over the head without covering the face intentionally. This design prevented excessive frost formation where the fur met the flesh. Although a lightly powdered frost occasionally formed on an uncovered face, it was easily brushed away. Conversely, completely covering the face lead to the formation of a layer of hard ice.

A parka's sleeves were long and wide, allowing the wearer to draw cold hands inside and warm them against the chest. This feature provided a practical solution for warming chilly hands in cold weather conditions. A parka could keep its wearer warm up to .

The thick hide of a krotter could be used to craft parkas.

Availability
 Parkas were an essential item for the Iulutiuns and the Nakulutiuns of the Great Glacier. Iulutiun parkas were made from caribou or seal skin, while Nakulutiun parkas were made from the fur and skin from bears, foxes and wolves.

Notable Wearers of Parkas
Classes
 Druids were known to use parkas.
 Parkas were commonly used by Ice Hunters
Individuals
 Vilheim Baltson often wore parkas.

## Paper money

Paper money was a form of currency widely used in Shou Lung.

Usage
Rather than being made of precious metals such as silver and gold, the notes were made of relatively worthless paper, and given value by the people's trust in the issuer. As such, only a truly well-organized and well-regarded realm would be able to make use of paper money to buttress their currency.

History
Paper money was originally invented in Shou Lung, and the empire was the largest user of paper currency in the Realms. Shou Lung maintained ch'ao paper notes as standard units of currency, issued in amounts of 1, 10, and 100 ch'ao. It was traditional for Shou people to offer up paper money to their ancestors by burning it on festival days along with paper clothes and food, so as to allow the venerated ancestor the luxuries of life.

Paper money found little use elsewhere in Kara-Tur. The rich but decentralized realm of T'u Lung minted its own metal coins, Kozakura freely used foreign coins, and most other neighboring lands used limited coinage or barter, with paper notes having no cachet.

Paper money found some use in the Hordelands. In the 1350s DR, Yamun Khahan issued paper notes in Quaraband, supplementing the traditional barter economy. Though his subjects were reluctant to use the paper money, it was used within his capital.

Paper notes rarely reached the opposite end of the continent, but were known of in Faerûn. Referred to as bela, it was functionally worthless and payment with it was used as a method of insult. Paper money's lack of value in Faerûn led to the saying "Buying Steelpense with Bela", indicating financial ineptitude. At best, the gaijin of Faerûn considered it an innocuous curiosity.

## Parsley

Parsley was a species of edible fragrant herb that received wide culinary usage across Faerûn. and beyond.

Availability
Parsley was cultivated for culinary use as far as the herb gardens of the hot lands of Zakhara.

A variety of elven blue-leaved parsley in Waterdeep was brought up and sold from Skullport by its drow merchants, but some locals confused that herb with parsley exported from Secomber.

Some mages knew an evocation spell that could create herbs and spices. This spell, spice, could conjure parsley, along with many other edible flavorings.

Usages
In the City of Splendors, parsley was often used as garnish. The Inn of the Dripping Dagger offered platters of bacon and bread rolls served on a bed of parsley. The well-known dish called Dragon Soup included parsley among its ingredients. Waterdhavian-based shops – Aurora's Emporium and Aurora's Whole Realms Catalogue, both offered parsley for sale among the common seasonings as well as parsley-flavored honey.

In Deepingdale, parsley and butter were used to cook carrots, such as by Korvan, a cook of The Rising Moon in the mid–14 century DR. Another dish from the same establishment that was cooked with parsley was the Ladies' Fowl, a variant of the Old Tower Fowl dish. In Tasseldale and Battledale, parsley was sometimes used in preparing broiled rabbit with brandy and plum sauce. Archenbridge's tavern called the Bridge and Bow offered bread, topped with herbed melted cheese and parsley butter to its guests. A very impressive dish called stuffed turkey pie, served at The Watchful Eye in Essembra, included parsley among its ingredients. Lastly, Boar and Chestnut Deep Pot, that was served at The Fall of Stars restaurant in Harrowdale, was made using parsley.

In Silverymoon, parsley-and-egg fingertarts were a common dish served at eveningfeasts. Parsley and garlic butter was a popular food from the village of Amphail. Further inland, the Maid of the Moors eatery in the village of Mornbryn's Shield served a fresh garden salad that included parsley among its many ingredients. The herb was also eaten in Westbridge, where it was used to flavor cream at The Wemic Comes to Westbridge restaurant. Beldar's Rest inn found in the village of Yartar prepared generously herbed potatoes, fried and topped with a white sauce of chives, sour cream, and parsley. And the city of Neverwinter's own Moonstone Mask served parsley-and-mint chicken broth.

Other places along the Sword Coast North where parsley was consumed included The Singing Sprite inn in Secomber, where flatbreads were topped with chopped greens and a sauce of cooked leeks, garlic, parsley vinegar, and beaten egg yolks.

Marsemban tarts were bite-sized pastries made with diced salmon and crab, sauced with a mix of almond milk, wine, leeks, and persimmons, all topped with parsley and potato. Blentra's Oysters and Wild Rice in Mushroom Soup, served at the Platter of Plenty in Marsember also included a handful of parsley per recipe. In Arabel, garlic-and-parsley butter was one of the staples of the Hungry Man eatery. And The Baron's Bottle tavern in Gladehap cooked up rabbit patè and topped it with chopped parsley and thyme to order.

The Old Wheel in Crimmor used parsley and fried ground-moss for a culinary presentation of soft-boiled quail eggs for its guests. The guest accommodations in Arnise Hold, just outside of Athkatla, offered parsley flavored water to its visitors. While The Board Laid Bare in Asbravn used parsley mostly as garnish.

In the neighboring nation of Tethyr, the Stag Triumphant, in the town of Mosstone, served its grand namesake dish – stag triumphant, atop several bucketfuls of parsley.

The temple and monastery of Chauntea in Veltalar brewed strong ale using fermented parsley, by some accords.

Elminster Aumar was partial to Marsemban dark spreads of almond butter, smeared on loaves of bread and sprinkled with parsley, chopped garlic, and dill.

Elven cuisine often used parsley along with many other, usually raw, vegetables.

Medicine
Some believed that parsley possessed blood-purifying properties.

## Paraster Montheir

Paraster Montheir was a trader from Athkatla in the mid-14 century DR.

Personality
He was audacious and arrogant.

Abilities
He had magic to call his own, including some nondescript shieldings which he trusted with his life.

Activities
He imported sweet wines from Lapaliiya.

Relationships
He commanded a significant number of guards, some of which wielded magic of their own.

Possessions
To Mirt's knowledge, Paraster had established three new trading companies, staffed by servants in his pay, to which he'd sold three tall-houses with shops, and all of which sent their profits to his own accounts.

History
While in Waterdeep in late, Paraster Montheir meant to evade payments to Mirt of Waterdeep for some services he implied were intended to be kept secret by Mirt. He had torn up his copy of the agreement, and believed he'd be hailed as a hero in Athkatla for giving Mirt the slip. Mirt reminded him there were still properties that could be seized by the law of Waterdeep, but Paraster proved unresponsive to the threat.

During their conversation, Asper announced to Mirt that Laeral was there to see him; Mirt told her that Laeral could see him, but only on her knees. She entered, begging for mercy, putting on a show for Paraster that Khelben Arunsun would do anything to square a debt he had, even allow him to mistreat Laeral. Paraster was suitably impressed, and relented on his intentions of leaving the debt unpaid. While he was leaving, Asper told him Maerilee Goodfellow's scars were not from pirates, but from Mirt's lashing; Ieiridauna Amalree let out a horrifying undead shriek, pretending to be Laeral's voice as Mirt tortured her for Khelben's alleged debt.

## Parhek Dynasty

Parhek was the last and least fortunate ruling dynasty of Amn, which lasted merely from 1237 DR to 1276 DR.

History
The family came to prominence in the  when King Belhinn Wavegold II died fighting the Black Horde. The crown passed through his only child Peldara to her husband Malek Parhek, making him the first king of the new dynasty. The new royal family had a brief and unfortunate existence as misfortune and conspiracy bedevilled them. Mercantile powers grew in strength under their reign, continually subverting and stressing the realm, and each king had but a short reign before they died young.

The situation came to a head in the  when King Dhanar was assassinated by the agents of ambitious merchants and the realm collapsed into civil war. His children were scattered to protect them, but Prince Dhanar was assassinated in Tethyr by the Eldreth Veluuthra, Princess Rasa was tracked down in Silverymoon by Amnian agents, and Prince Malek was finally located in Waterdeep. The final Parhek died in 1306 DR at the hands of the manipulator Arlen Bormul, who poisoned Prince Rohav after exhausting his usefulness as a tool.

The period of Amn's internal conflict only ended in 1333 DR, when Thayze Selemchant halted the Great Amnian Trade War and unified the land under the Council of Six.

## Path of wild magic

The path of wild magic was a primal path that some barbarians followed, that saw them receive influence from powerful magic that often originated from other planes of existence.

Description
As most barbarians lived deep in the wilds of nature, they became more susceptible to the influence of wild magic. Over the years, living in remote locales that were influenced by energies from the Feywild, or even the Upper planes, they accumulated supernatural powers that were often practiced by their ancient forebears.

Culture
Aasimar, genasi, tiefling, and other planetouched individuals were most among those barbarians that followed the path of wild magic.

Abilities
By their very nature, wild magic barbarians garnered greater awareness of magic and dweomers that remained nearby. One or more times per day, they could detect the location of any magical spell within  of their person, and even determine the school of magic from which it originated.

As they honed their abilities, barbarians that walked the path could tap into wild magic whenever they entered into their rage. Surprising and sometimes catastrophous events could occur as a result of their wild magic surges. They could just as easily conjure the spirit of a flumph as they could imbue their weapon with magical force energy. Skilled barbarians could even harness this power to bolster the combat prowess of themselves or their allies.

Some of the most well-traveled and venerated barbarians became so in-tune with wild magic, that the inherent magical energy within them would lash out if they ever became truly in danger.

Yet it was only the most-experienced wild magic barbarians who could truly control and harness their wild magic surges. Whenever they raged or faced true peril, they could deliberately conjure protective lights around their body, teleport themselves away, shoot out bolts of lightning, or manifest other wild magic feats.

## Pammella Nortale

Hostel Mother Pammella Nortale was the proprietress of Nortale's Hostel, a non-profit hostel in the Baldur's Gate/Lower City of Baldur's Gate in the late 15 century DR.

Description
Pammella was a female hin with light skin, blonde hair and blue eyes. She usually wore common garb such as plain tunics and tan cloth breeches.

History
By the time the Absolute crisis brought war to the city of Baldur's Gate in the, Hostel Mother Pammella Nortale was its only manager, employee, and the owner. She ran the lodgings as a non-profit refugee shelter as the city was flooded by the people fleeing the encroaching army. The halfling matron was struggling and sought financing and donors among the nobility of the city. She penned a letter to Lord Whitburn, who once claimed that he'd love to donate if only he had funds. Hostel Mother pointed out the Lord's recent marriage and coming into money and openly asked for the promised large donation. Some members of the nobility were also admirers of Pammella Nortale and her work. Young Derque Rillyn was a regular sponsor but could not make donations above his regular amounts, as per his father Hir Rillyn's instructions. However, Derque suggested renaming the lodgings to Hir Rillyn's Hostel, as that would likely encourage the noble to finance it.

## Patriars, A Homegrown Aristocracy

Patriars, A Homegrown Aristocracy was a journalistic work by some brave or possibly suicidal writer who shared no love for the patriar families. The book offered savage takedowns and criticisms aimed at every patriar family of the Upper City, including House Caldwell, that was accused of hoarding patronage of Baldurian museums and galleries, House Irlentree with their impressive fleets of merchant vessels, and House Ravenshade, who usurped the ink, dye, jewelry, and gem industries in the city. Patriars, A Homegrown Aristocracy was filled with acidic insults and disdain for each of the noble houses, celebrating each family's bad luck and financial failures over the years.

By the, several copies of Patriars, A Homegrown Aristocracy were in rotation in the city of Baldur's Gate. One could be found in the printing cellar of Baldur's Mouth and another was sold by Nansi Gretta in The Bibliophile book shop in the Lower City.

## Paereth Vandurn

Activities
Paereth Vandurn was a swordcaptain with the Purple Dragons.

History
Paereth Vandurn was keeping watch at Castle Obarskyr in Suzail at some point after Mirtul 26 of the, before the Council of the Dragon. When the fugitives Storm Silverhand and Elminster Aumar tried to sneak into the castle disguised as the Royal Magician Ganrahast and Lord Warder Vainrence, Paereth Vandurn was fooled by their disguises, but Rorskryn Mreldrake spotted them, and pointed out the deception to the swordcaptain. He agreed to hold the two until they could be examined; the two didn't fight.

Vandurn and his soldiers escorted the men to a holding room to wait; the swordcaptain received a few verbal jabs from the imposters, and a few snickers from his own men throughout the process, which he handled with some difficulty because of the confusing situation. However, before they made it to their intended location, some sort of distant magical backlash affected Storm Silverhand and Elminster, stunning all nearby soldiers, including him. The fugitives made their escape in the immediate aftermath.

## Parzal the Outrageous

Parzal the Outrageous was a powerful arcanist and cleric of ancient Netheril who owned the QM III business in Eileanar and narrowly avoided its destruction in .

Description
Parzal had piercing blue eyes and black hair that he kept in the latest fashionable styles. He kept a youthful appearance and was 6'2" (1.88 meters) tall and weighed .

Relationships
Parzal's father was a ranger who had traveled from another world, and his mother was a spellcaster from the far east of Faerûn. He had a younger brother, Lampris. Parzal never took a partner or had children of his own.

His closest friends were Archmeagan and Mortimar, though only Archmeagan proved a reliable friend.

History
When Parzal was only five years old, his family home was attacked by a sohei from the Monastery of the West Wind. The sohei slew his parents, robbed and burned the home, and kidnapped the two children. Feeling that Parzal was too strong-willed to be controlled, the sohei abandoned him in the Dunes of Death and eventually sold Lampris into slavery.

Parzal wandered the desert for seven months before being found by monks who rescued him and adopted him into their monastery. He became a full brother of the monastery by age 25, and on his 30 birthday the monks revealed that many years ago they had gotten word of a young child with great mystical potential and power. They had sent their sohei to recover the boy and test his ability by leaving him in the desert, where he would be eventually found and adopted. In payment, the sohei was granted the rights to everything he could find within Parzal's home.

Shocked and horrified by the revelation, Parzal fled the monastery, swore to avenge his family, and searched the world for his lost brother, though Lampris was never found again. Crushed by disillusionment, Parzal eventually made his way to Netheril and took up the art of magic. He also wrote his autobiographical book, Parzal's Perils, and set up an initial transcription of 1,000 books for sale. They rapidly sold out, and one reader, Mortimar, offered to help him set up a business together that could sell more of the books as well as magical reagents and spell components. Such was the strength of demand for Parzal's Perils, the pair opened up a total of seven stores within a year, with QM III in Eileanar being the third.

As the business matured, Parzal turned to writing his books and researching spells full-time, leaving the management of the businesses entirely to Mortimar. This arrangement was interrupted by a special request that Parzal received—a bespoke curse to be delivered to a man who had acted immorally with a married woman. The payment of 100,000 platinum pieces was more than enough to secure Parzal's interest, and after a search of Netheril he laid the horrific curse upon the target. The victim turned out to be an official of the Eileanar government, and this capital crime led to Parzal being sought-after by law enforcement across Netheril.

After fleeing to QM III Parzal, explained the situation to Mortimar, who came up with a plan to fake Parzal's death via an ambiguous magical accident. The constabulary were successfully misled by Mortimar's discovery of a body with a broken amulet of life protection, and the business ownership was transferred from Parzal to his surviving partner. Only then did Mortimar gloat in Parzal's face about how he had set the whole situation up, stolen the business, and made Parzal a fugitive. Parzal fled in secrecy and began work on the fearsome Parzal's revenge curse, labeling the finished scroll a "Promotion to Vitality and Stamina."

He returned to QM III in the guise of a poor magically illiterate fighter who sought to sell the loot he found. Mortimar eagerly bought the scroll for 50 gp, but found that, rather than improving his constitution, it withered him to a shadow of his former self. While Mortimar was incapacitated, Parzal's close friend Archmeagan took over the business for a time. Wracked by the torment of his existence, Mortimar falsely confessed to the cursing of the Karsus Enclave official and earned a quick execution. With his name cleared, Parzal revealed himself and was sentenced to a much milder 60 days in prison for faking his death.

Swearing to never let another person control his business, Parzal first took a long vacation somewhere south of Netheril. Unlike so many of his fellow arcanists, Parzal escaped Karsus's Folly by sheer chance.

## Pale reaver

Pale reavers were bloodthirsty undead spirits that arose from humanoids who had been killed and cursed for betraying people or factions that trusted them.

Description
Their cursed bodies were lightweight and hovered just above the ground. They were semi-corporeal, existing somewhere between solid and insubstantial. More powerful pale reavers could temporarily become completely insubstantial to phase through solid objects.

Pale reavers were known to disguise themselves with illusions to appear as living creatures, and often opted to appear as they did in life. They could mimic normal movement, although they could not perfectly hide their unnatural lightness nor their inherent eeriness. They were distinguished by their mane of long hair, which was white if they had not recently fed on blood but turned red when they did. It stayed red for several hours, and was apparent even when they disguised themselves.

Personality
Pale reavers were treacherous and angry. They were jealous of the living, and sought to trick, deceive, and feed on them. Even the weakest pale reavers—known as "creepers"—were intelligent by the standards of undead creatures, and the most powerful among them—known as pale reaver "lords"—were highly cunning and exceled at trickery.

Only the most powerful of pale reavers were able to retain their souls and the memories of their lives before undeath. Weaker pale reavers retained only fragments of their former selves.

Combat
All pale reavers—from the weakest creepers to the mightiest lords—sought to use stealth and guile to gain advantage over their foes. They kept their distance in combat, and would try to pick off injured or poorly armored enemies first. While the weakest (and least intelligent) pale reavers were apt to defensively stalk their prey and attack only when a good opportunity appeared, more powerful (and more intelligent) pale reavers would tempt foes into compromising situations and would delight in misleading and tricking them. They would use their ability to become insubstantial in order to make enemies waste their resources and attacks, or to flee if needed.

When a pale reaver attacked, its long hair stretched out up to  to strike like vampiric tendrils. When struck, victims were immobilized and the pale reaver would begin to drain their blood through these hairs. Draining blood in this manner restored the pale reaver's strength and turned its hair red. They used the long reach of their hair to keep foes at a distance while healing their own injuries with blood.

Powerful pale reaver lords had the ability to paralyze others with a terrifying shriek, and to dominate with their gaze.

As undead creatures, pale reavers were not susceptible to disease or poison. They were resistant to necrotic energy but harmed by radiant energy.

Society
Pale reavers were predators of living creatures, and would lure them to their doom. A favorite tactic was to use their illusory disguises to entice a victim into a remote location before attacking suddenly.

All but the most powerful pale reavers haunted the places where they had been killed for their crimes, and could not venture more than a few miles from that location. Pale reaver lords, however, could freely travel in search of blood.

Despite their treacherous nature, pale reavers were known to associate with other undead creatures. They often sought to ally with large and physically mighty undead, with lesser pale reaver creepers joining bands of similarly bloodthirsty monsters—like ghouls—while pale reaver lords might form their own organizations or recruit evil creatures—such as poltergeists, skeletons, vampires, wights, and wraiths—to act as guards, soldiers, or servants. Pale reavers could be found among the undead inhabitants of the city of Evernight in the Shadowfell as well as among the undead ranks of Thay during the late 15 century DR.

History
A pale reaver lord who appeared as a beautiful female elf was part of the Thayan occupation of Undumor in the late 15 century DR.

When members of the White Petals—a gang that conspired against Clan Neng of Nathlan—were slain in, some arose as pale reaver creepers and continued to follow the commands of their old leader, Mad Fox.

## Painspeaker

Painspeakers were undead creatures that wielded truespeak. What all of them had in common was that they were in life a person who had an unspoken message to relay.

Description
Painspeakers appeared as near-skeletal humans, with grayish flesh pulled taut across their bones. Painspeakers resembled the creatures they were when they were alive. While their appearance was fearful, they became truly horrid when they spoke with their inhuman, gravely voices. Each syllable they uttered was rife with visceral evil, with the undercurrent of some ancient forgotten language beneath their words.

Personality
Painspeakers were created from those that died before their message was delivered, whether they were a courier or a lover who never professed their love. They rose because of the words they had never uttered, and were cursed to blight others with their truename magic.

History
Painspeakers had a long history of being connected to important events.

Abilities
Instead of employing a broad selection of truename utterances, a painspeaker uttered only the reversed form of the moderate word of nurturing but could do so all day, two or three times a minute. They typically limited their physical attacks, actively uttering the recitation of the fortified state to improve their defenses between utterances of pain, swinging sucker punches at creatures that lowered their guard to attack or to flee.

In addition to their truespeech utterings, painspeakers possessed darkvision and other traits common to the undead.

Known Painspeakers
 Erdwin the Silent, who rose as a Painspeaker

## Pale master

Pale masters were powerful necromancy spellcasters who used their knowledge of the arcane to guide them into the divine deathless arts that strengthened their bodies with undead powers and allowed them to control undead creatures. They arose from the likes of mages, sorcerers, wizard-clerics, and occasionally bards.

Activities
Almost all pale masters were wizards or sorcerers in the past who could cast the command undead and vampiric touch spells, were well-versed in religion, and all of the pale masters were creatures of evil, either who followed law, chaos, or neither. Those mages who wanted to embark un such a dark path had to lock themselves in a tomb for three days with animated undead creatures. Whether the mage interacted with the undead peacefully or violently, after three days, they received a divine epiphany that allowed them to become pale masters. Those wizards who died whilst in the tomb could still become pale masters afterward if they were resurrected or raised by other means.

Abilities
By learning the dark magics, pale masters learned to control droves of lesser undead and summon more powerful undead creatures under their control. Many pale masters visually looked a lot like their undead servants, with their bodies changing and fusing with the magics of the undeath. While they were originally mages, pale masters became more focused on close combat, learning to use light and medium armor.

Pale masters learned to summon such undead creature as shadows, greater shadows, wraiths, dread wraiths, spectres, ghouls, ghasts, wights, and powerful pale master mummies. The most powerful and experienced pale masters, epic pale masters, gained the ability to cast certain epic magic spells and summon immensely powerful creatures. These creatures were vampires, greater bodaks, ghoul kings, skeleton blackguards, liches, lich lords, alhoons, alhoon elders, and lesser demiliches.

In addition to new undead summoning powers, pale masters eventually learned to animate dead and gained darkvision. Those who already possessed darkvision prior to becoming pale masters had their field of view in the dark expanded to . Their affinity undead armor and control undead spells, empowering them. With time, pale masters achieved deathless vigor and later became tough as bone, with their bodies becoming more akin to that of the undead, boosting their fortitude, granting them immunity to diseases, and rendering them unable to be stunned. A strange necrophiliac urge came after that. Pale masters removed at least one of their limbs and grafted an undead creature's limb in its place, boosting their strength and giving them the horrible ability to paralyze, wither, degenerate, and destroy creatures with a touch. The most powerful pale masters created an undead cohort who shared a part of their master's powers. Lastly, pale masters bodies became mummified without turning them into actual undead, making them immune to poison, sleep, paralysis, death magic, ability drain, and energy drain, and prevented enemies from sapping their strength, dexterity, and constitution. Despite being so similar to undead, pale masters still had to breathe, eat, and sleep.

Notable Pale Masters
Ilivarra, a drow wizard-pale mistress who operated in the Sunset Mountains in the late 14 century DR.

## Paeregur Amaethur Donlas

Paeregur Amaethur Donlas was a warrior in the Frostfire Banner adventuring party during the mid–8 century DR.

Description
His voice was deeper than that of his two comrades, Arvas and Faldast.

Possessions
At the time of his death, he had a sword, a new helm, new armor, and an elaborate codpiece.

History
Paeregur was a member of the Frostfire Banner when they took on High Duke Horostos's assignment to search for the reason why all animals larger than one's hand, as well as most people, were vanishing on the road between Starmantle and Ripplestones.

They were ambushed on their first night out of Starmantle by a chiming mist, the Slayer. Rolian and Paeregur noticed the chiming in the night. Paeregur went off to check up on Avras, who was on watch, and found him turned into a husk that collapsed into dust at the slightest touch. Rolian was attacked in the same way right there. He cried out to wake up the rest, and regrouped with Lhaerand and Phostral; the mist did the same thing to Phostral, and Lhaerand jumped off a cliff rather than be slain. Paeregur was last, and the mist killed him slowly, taking one limb at a time.

## Parchment

Parchment was a high-quality writing surface made from animal hide, capable of holding ink, paint, and other pigments.

Description

Parchment was similar to other writing materials like vellum, paper, and papyrus, and was relatively commonplace. It was made of animal hide, often goat or sheepskin.

Availability

Aurora's Whole Realms Catalogue offered high-quality parchment sheets for 1 gp each, alongside various other writing surfaces and tools. It also sold packs of 50 sheets of gridded parchment for 45 gp, for use in mapmaking.

In Waterdeep, the League of Skinners and Tanners handled all aspects of tanning and leatherworking, including the production of parchment.

Calaunt was a great producer and exporter of leatherwork including parchment.

Usage
Projects such as building plans, maps, academic treatises, and wizard's tomes used parchment scrolls and books.

Parchment was a fine material for creating spell scrolls, producing more reliable results than papyrus, though not as well as vellum.

Spell Components
 Rope trick used a twist of parchment.
 Shield of faith used a small piece of parchment with holy text.
 Ventriloquism used a piece of parchment rolled into a cone.

## Pauli Kidd

Pauli Kidd also known as Patpahootie, is an Australian writer of fantasy fiction.

Career
Before pursuing a career in writing, Kidd worked at Beam Software, an Australia-based game developer. She is credited as, among other things, the writer, director and lead designer of Nightshade, an action-adventure title for the NES. She is also credited as the lead designer for Shadowrun.

Kidd's first book, entitled Mus of Kerbridge, was released in 1995 and received a short-list nomination for the 1995 Aurealis Awards best fantasy novel. She since released six more standalone novels, wrote a two-book series, and contributed to two of Dungeons & Dragons novel series, Forgotten Realms: The Nobles and Greyhawk. Her novel releases include White Plume Mountain (WotC), Descent into the Depths of the Earth (WotC), A Whisper of Wings (Vision), The Rats of Acomar (Vision), The Fangs of K'aath (United Press UK), Lilith (Vision), Petal Storm (Vision), Neue Europa (Vision), and the Petal Storm graphic novel series. Kidd also has written a non-fiction strategy guide based on the video game Discworld II: Missing Presumed...!? and has written two short stories which were published in Dragon magazine.

Kidd lives in Perth, Australia.

Works
 The Council of Blades

## Parley Pool

Parley Pool was a harbor located in Skullport.

Description
Parley Pool was located on the southwestern coast of Skull Island. The area surrounding Parley Pool featured a pebble-strewn beach that gently sloped toward the water. During high tide, the waters of the pool rose to the very walls of the Skull Island fortress, temporarily submerging the beach. After the tides receded, fortunate individuals occasionally discovered valuable items washed ashore. These items were believed to originate from the mysterious shipwrecks in the South Seacaves, transported by the flow through the magical locks.

History
The name 'Parley Pools' originated from an incident involving an audacious Luskanite who insisted on conducting his business during a time when the Skulls of Skullport had forbidden all commerce in Skullport. As the Luskanite vehemently protested from the deck of his ship, several flame cannons unexpectedly turned on the vessel and unleashed a destructive barrage. The ship was obliterated, and all those on board perished. Since that fateful event, vessels arriving at Parley Pool did so with their armaments lowered, adhering to proper decorum to avoid a similar fate.

## Pâté

Pâté was a variety of a dish, usually served as a smallbite, made using a wide variety of ingredients and mystery meats in a mashed spreadable form.

Availability
Pâté made out of various ingredients was commonly served in Cormyr. Namely, pâté of dubious ingredients was available from The Wight on a Waterdragon in the coastal city of Marsember as well as The Moon and Stars of Waymoot, where it was spread over hot buns. Suzail's Thistleflame Keep also served pâté to its guests, made of liver and stuffed inside baked mushrooms. Goose liver was another meat used in pâté preparation, as well as rabbit, as served in Hot Highsun Handbreads of Gladehap.

Similarly, fowl pâté was served with slabs of bread, sausage, and cheese at The Bowels of the Earth in Waterdeep.

In Ravens Bluff, the novelty dish called cat's liver was, in reality, just a simple liver pâté, smothered over crackers. In the city of Calaunt in the Vast, softmeat was the term used for spiced pâté of fowl liver and sold as a street food.

History
The Ancient Nubari of the Malatran Plateau, many centuries before the 14 century DR, used spelljammer trade to import luxurious foodstuffs like pate, truffle, and caviar from off-world.

## Panchengjuduohuh

Panchengjuduohuh  was a magical city located in caves beneath Durkon in T'u Lung. It was a city of portals that connected to places across Kara-Tur, Faerûn, and beyond.

Description

Panchengjuduohuh was situated at the mouth of the River Hidden, a subterranean tributary of the Fenghsintzu River. The river began at a massive fountain-like jet at the center of a lake on which Panchengjuduohuh had its docks. By river, the city was protected by the leopard-shaped Living Gate, a magical gateway. The Living Gate controlled who could enter the city, and only allowed entrance to followers of Mad Monkey, the Black Leopard Cult, Lung Jua, or any enemies of Lung Jua.

The interior of the cave that contained Panchengjuduohuh was dome-shaped and lit by bright magical lights in the stone wall. The city itself consisted of many old buildings that appeared as ruins. Each building was nothing more than a series of walls and doors. The doors could be opened as portals to other parts of Toril, other worlds of the material plane, and the outer planes. Each door would stay open for 10 hours, before closing itself with a slam. The destinations of the portals were unpredictable and prone to changing. They were also invisible from the other side, but would become visible if the spell dimension door was cast on them.

Government
Panchengjuduohuh was protected by the Ministry of Planar Adventures, a part of the Celestial Bureaucracy.

History
In, the pagoda temple of the Black Leopard Cult was sent deep into the earth from Durkon by the forces of Wo Can, who used a talisman of pure good. It sank down through the earth and arrived in Panchengjuduohuh.

By, the pagoda was the base of operations for Lung Jua. Lung Jua used the magic of the city to drain the life force of his followers into a large statue of a demon. This left the portals of Panchengjuduohuh in an inert state, where they would connect only to other doors in the city.  Lung Jua's plan was to finally inhabit the statue himself, and ascend to ultimate power. His plan was countered by students of Mad Monkey, who had been sent up the River Hidden to stop him. The epic battle that followed threatened to destroy Panchengjuduohuh.

 Sigil, another 'city of doors'.

## Palul

Palul was a Nexalan village near Nexal in Maztica.

Geography
Palul was located in green terraced mountains to the northeast of Nexal. Although Nexal was not visible directly from Palul, a journey around one of its neighboring mountains allowed an observer to see the city. The wilderness to the east of the village was inhabited by Kultakans.

Description
A small pyramid dedicated to Zaltec stood in the middle of the village.

Economy
Farmers grew maize on the slopes and in the valley. Many colorful birds, such as parrots and macaws made their home in the area; their feathers were used by local featherworkers for pluma magic.

History
Erixitl of Palul lived in this village as a child before she was captured by Kultakans and sold into slavery around 1351 DR. Although unknown to Erixitl at the time, this action was actually the beginning of a divine plan by the god Qotal to thwart the schemes that some evil deities had for Maztica.

In the, Cordell and the Golden Legion from Amn arrived in Maztica and, after defeating other Maztican nations, he led his force to march on Nexal. Their route took them through Palul. Naltecona the ruler of Nexal, had heard of the Legion's victories over the Payit and the Kultakans. Rather than fight them directly, he decided to organize a trap for them in Palul. He organized a large banquet to be given in honur of the foreigners in Palul, while at the same time hiding a large force of Nexalan warriors within and around the village. Cordell, upon arriving in Palul, accepted the invitation to the banquet, falsely believing that this was a genuine welcome to their coming. However, his mage Darien discovered the ruse as they were feasting and alerted Cordell to the betrayal. Cordell then gave orders to his men and their Maztican allies. They drew weapons and attacked the Nexalans before the Nexalans could strike first. The Legion and their Maztican allies overwhelmed the surprised Nexalans and decisively defeated them, slaughtered most of the inhabitants of Palul, and then looted and razed the village.

Following this, the Legion continued their march on Nexal. The events in Palul, however, convinced Naltecona that it was futile to fight the foreigners and led him to make the decision to surrender Nexal to them without resistance.

Notable Inhabitants
 Erixitl
 Zitli
 Shatil

## Pain and Suffering mantle

The Pain and Suffering mantle was a psionic mantle that granted ardents and divine minds an array of psionic powers and abilities derived from the universal concepts of pain and suffering.

Deities

Abilities
Those who followed Pain and Suffering mantle gained the unique psionic ability to expend inflict half the pain dealt to them by an attack to their attacker. Doing so required the expenditure of a psionic focus.

The psychic aura exuded by divine minds of the Pain and Suffering mantle made the manifester and any allies within the radius of their aura deal more grevious wounds to the injured.

Powers
Novice manifesters gained the powers disable, inflict pain, and recall agony. Manifesters of some experience could transfer or share their pain to others with empathic feedback, forced share pain, and hostile empathic transfer. As a manifester progressed further they could send people into a frenzy with psychotic break and turn creatures into suffering masses with fuse flesh. Masters of this mantle obtained pain affinity field, allowing them to create an affinity feedback loop of pain with nearby creatures.

## Paranoia

Paranoia was a strong feeling of being persecuted or threatened by others. In some cases, it could extend beyond a specific perception of danger and come to encompass a vast and vague conspiracy turned against the person.

Acquisition
 The gem of delusion could inflict a random mental illness upon a victim, including an overpowering and severe form of paranoia.
 The jewels of ardor, imbued with the avarice of Graz'zt, cultivated a feeling of overwhelming greed, envy, and paranoia in those exposed to the crystals. Dwarves were especially strongly affected by this.
 Contact with demonically-affected chardalyn could cause general paranoia. In particular, a chardalyn figurehead of Errtu, found in Lac Dinneshere, caused uncontrollable paranoia in those that touched it regarding an imagined cult devoted to Auril.
 Dragon bone stew gave the drinker the greed and paranoia appropriate to a great dragon.
 Arcane blight caused intense feelings of paranoia before transforming someone into a nothic.

Effects

While in a state of elevated paranoia, the victim was overcome by delusions of being targeted by (real or imagined) individuals, groups, or the gods themselves. They might even adopt delusional and megalomaniacal beliefs about themselves that explained this apparent malice and jealousy. The evidence for this could be based on misinterpretation or simply invented, and it was difficult or impossible to dissuade these suspicions.

In many cases, a paranoid individual believed that their reputation, wealth, or position was in danger, but this could easily extend to physical threat. They might regard everyone with distrust, with even innocuous conversation and laughter between friends and strangers coming under suspicion of hiding something malevolent. They could respond to this feeling of danger by ensuring their own safety materially, such as by hiring guards, checking food and drink for poison, reinforcing their home with new locks and traps, and other security measures. In the most extreme of cases, overwhelming paranoia could lead someone to abandon or betray their friends, family, and allies due to perceived threats, possibly even trying to kill them first, either directly or by agents.

As befitting their grand and terrible interests, unhinged wizards like necromancers and alienists had a habit of extending the scope of their paranoid delusions to include rival archmages, angry gods, and strange alien planar nemeses. This resulted in similar interpersonal issues as other paranoids, but with more portals and fireballs.

Notable Affected
 Most beholders had an unreasonably deep well of xenophobia and paranoia between their two minds that fuelled their malevolence.
 Dragons were quite mentally stable, but weaker evil dragons were especially prone to falling to paranoia as they imagined countless threats.
 The god Cyric was notably paranoid (among his many other problems), and this often influenced the behavior of his priests and followers.
 Brains in jars were particularly vulnerable to mental illness, including paranoia, dementia, and schizophrenia.
 Avarice, the tiefling evoker, was intensely paranoid and trusted none but her servant gargoyles.
 Darribeth Meltimer, a mage adventurer, suffered from paranoia and hallucinations induced by the perils of Undermountain and the malevolent quasit Teeha.
 Dradeel, a man who developed traumatized paranoia about werewolves after the trauma of being shipwrecked on Werewolf Island and then being imprisoned and experimented on in Spellhold.
 Durlag Trollkiller, builder of Durlag's Tower, fell into paranoia and retreated to his fortress, seeking to protect his treasures. He lurked alone in the trap-filled tower until he died.
 Gromnir Il-Khan, a bhaalspawn warlord, became extremely paranoid while besieged in Saradush. He kept himself and his soldiers in his fortress for fear of Melissan and other Bhaalspawn (he was right).
 Hiflanyl, an elf trapped in Nalavara's hoard demiplane, became extremely paranoid of imagined threats including his own companions, and would regularly hide from everyone with invisibility.
 Kalain, an artist in Waterdeep, became an isolated, misanthropic wreck after Dagult Neverember broke her heart and ruined her life, believing that all strangers were his agents sent to kill her.
 Salitre, an inmate who became obsessed with protecting himself with traps while in Wheloon Prison.
 Shangalar the Black was an exceptionally paranoid wizard who worked to defend himself from the entire world.
 Toramir Kuurgan, former naval commander of Tethyr, became a paranoid and ruthless outlaw after decades among pirates.
 Xardorok Sunblight, the duergar warlord, fell to paranoia and obsession from his exposure to the fell energies of chardalyn.

## Pappilon

Pappilon was an adventuring human, active in the late 14 century DR.

Description
Pappilon was a human man with a light skin tone and eflin features. He kepts his head clean-shaven and wore light and unarmored garb.

History
In Kythorn of the, Waterdeep – the City of Splendors, became once again menaced by the drow from the mega-dungeon of Undermountain. The Masked Lords determined that the danger was coming from underneath the Yawning Portal, and Durnan, the proprietor of the famed establishment, put out a call for adventurers willing to traverse the dungeon's depths and route the evil within.

Pappilon was among the heroes who answered the call, eager to spread the faith of his goddess and to practice his healing arts. LaFontaine was present at the Yawning Portal's common room, when a squadron of drow assassins sent by "queen" Valsharess attacked. The dark elves slaughtered many of the gathered adventurers. Survivors chased the assassins down into the Undermountain, while those killed were resurrected by the city's clergy, including Pappilon.

Palisade, the Ward of Karliath was a powerful magical large shield found in the ruins of Myth Drannor.

Description
The shield was generated from unholy energy into a form of steel and dragonscale, but in a peculiar twist of fate it took the appearance of a typical relic of Myth Drannor; an ornate silvery elven shield decorated with leafy designs.

Powers
Palisade's powers were drawn from the red dragon dracolich Karliath. It made the bearer much more durable and resilient, and absorbed any curses thrown their way. It also gave them a touch of supernatural mobility, letting them dodge more easily even in heavy armor.

This shield was created as a by-product of the necromantic rituals and undead energies tied to the dracolich Karliath. ## Pact of Eschatos

The Pact of Eschatos, also known as the Declaration of Eschatos, was an ancient agreement or accord between the drow of the Underdark and the fomorians of the Feydark.

Description
A core tenet of the Pact involved cyclopes loyal to the fomorians allying with and accompanying drow on small-scale raids and evil schemes, although such cooperation rarely lasted for long. The details of the Pact were otherwise unknown to outsiders, although it was rumored that it was meant to safeguard some kind of hidden lore.

History
Whenever drow and cyclopes were seen working together, rumors about the Pact invariably began to resurface.

In the mid-to-late 15 century DR, Prince Araithe of Sarifal and his drow allies held hostage a fomorian princess named Marabaldia for ten years to force concessions relating to the Pact of Eschatos, which included lending cyclopes to Araithe for his schemes. One such cyclops was Borgol, who gave his life while helping Marabaldia to escape her captivity in the late 1470s DR.
